People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. It's just strange, you know? Some days I forget lunch completely. I'll just be sitting in my armchair—it's this lovely floral pattern, my daughter bought it for me back in, oh, must have been 2015 when she was still living in Ohio before her husband got transferred. They have such terrible winters there, I don't know how they stood it. But anyway, I'll just forget to eat. And then other days I stand at the kitchen counter just eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.

Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? It's a lovely mint green color. I bought it at Sears back when they still had that big catalog. Oh, I used to love looking through the Sears Wish Book right before Christmas when I was little. My brother and I would circle the things we wanted with a red crayon. He always wanted those heavy metal Tonka trucks, the bright yellow ones. They were practically indestructible, not like the cheap plastic toys they make for kids today... 

But, oh, what were we saying? Right, my weight. I just don't know for sure. Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I might have lost a few pounds, or maybe the sweets balanced it out. I just know my eating is all mixed up.

they can’t follow what I mean.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: I catch myself lining up papers and pill bottles over and over on the kitchen table, tapping each stack a certain number of times, even when I know I’m supposed to be getting ready to leave.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most mornings I wake up with a heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to look forward to watercolor class, quilting circle, and book club, but now I often leave the bag by the door and just can’t make myself care enough to go.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: My body feels slowed down lately, like it takes me twice as long to cross the room or button my sweater, though sometimes I pace around the house with the same papers in my hand.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I sit there staring at simple things, like whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first, and my mind gets so foggy that I end up doing neither.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but I do find myself thinking that if I didn’t wake up one morning, it might be a relief to everyone.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I am in that low, colorless state, I am also most caught up in the idea that the family papers and hospital notes are carrying hidden messages for me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, the sadness and loss of interest have taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill, even when the record worries were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had eased enough that I was eating and getting dressed more normally, but I still felt sure the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped teaching my craft classes and have missed calls, appointments, and family tasks I used to handle carefully. I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely more on reminders and put off things that used to be routine.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, since my sister’s health took that serious turn.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The ideas about hidden messages in the family and medical papers started about a year and a month ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. It's just strange, you know? Some days I forget lunch completely. I'll just be sitting in my armchair—it's this lovely floral pattern, my daughter bought it for me back in, oh, must have been 2015 when she was still living in Ohio before her husband got transferred. They have such terrible winters there, I don't know how they stood it. But anyway, I'll just forget to eat. And then other days I stand at the kitchen counter just eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy baking or has your interest in it changed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? It's a lovely mint green color. I bought it at Sears back when they still had that big catalog. Oh, I used to love looking through the Sears Wish Book right before Christmas when I was little. My brother and I would circle the things we wanted with a red crayon. He always wanted those heavy metal Tonka trucks, the bright yellow ones. They were practically indestructible, not like the cheap plastic toys they make for kids today... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Right, my weight. I just don't know for sure. Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I just know my eating is all mixed up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... 

But anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.

Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.

me twice as long to cross the room or button my sweater, though sometimes I pace around the house with the same papers in my hand.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I sit there staring at simple things, like whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first, and my mind gets so foggy that I end up doing neither.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but I do find myself thinking that if I didn’t wake up one morning, it might be a relief to everyone.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I am in that low, colorless state, I am also most caught up in the idea that the family papers and hospital notes are carrying hidden messages for me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, the sadness and loss of interest have taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill, even when the record worries were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had eased enough that I was eating and getting dressed more normally, but I still felt sure the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped teaching my craft classes and have missed calls, appointments, and family tasks I used to handle carefully. I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely more on reminders and put off things that used to be routine.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, since my sister’s health took that serious turn.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The ideas about hidden messages in the family and medical papers started about a year and a month ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... 

But, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...

But, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.

Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...

But anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.

And then other days I stand at the kitchen counter just eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy baking or has your interest in it changed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? It's a lovely mint green color. I bought it at Sears back when they still had that big catalog. Oh, I used to love looking through the Sears Wish Book right before Christmas when I was little. My brother and I would circle the things we wanted with a red crayon. He always wanted those heavy metal Tonka trucks, the bright yellow ones. They were practically indestructible, not like the cheap plastic toys they make for kids today... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Right, my weight. I just don't know for sure. Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I might have lost a few pounds, or maybe the sweets balanced it out. I just know my eating is all mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most mornings I wake up with a heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... 

But, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.

Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... 

But no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.

Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I just know my eating is all mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most mornings I wake up with a heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... 

But, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. It's just strange, you know? Some days I forget lunch completely. I'll just be sitting in my armchair—it's this lovely floral pattern, my daughter bought it for me back in, oh, must have been 2015 when she was still living in Ohio before her husband got transferred. They have such terrible winters there, I don't know how they stood it. But anyway, I'll just forget to eat. And then other days I stand at the kitchen counter just eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy baking or has your interest in it changed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? It's a lovely mint green color. I bought it at Sears back when they still had that big catalog. Oh, I used to love looking through the Sears Wish Book right before Christmas when I was little. My brother and I would circle the things we wanted with a red crayon. He always wanted those heavy metal Tonka trucks, the bright yellow ones. They were practically indestructible, not like the cheap plastic toys they make for kids today... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Right, my weight. I just don't know for sure. Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I might have lost a few pounds, or maybe the sweets balanced it out. I just know my eating is all mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I know I'm sounding like a broken record here, doctor, but it really is all the time. Like I've been saying, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just doesn't leave. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... 

But no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.
