So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this heavy, low feeling you have is something that you experience all the time, or are there times when you don't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: Well, I know I'm sounding like a broken record here, doctor, but it really is all the time. Like I've been saying, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just doesn't leave. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely more on reminders and put off things that used to be routine.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, since my sister’s health took that serious turn.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The ideas about hidden messages in the family and medical papers started about a year and a month ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any periods of at least two months where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no. No two months without it. Like I said, it's just always there, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. A whole year and then some. It feels like a lot longer, though. Time is funny like that, isn't it? When you're waiting for something, it drags, but when you look back, it seems to fly by. Like how fast the seasons change. I was just looking at the calendar in my kitchen—the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, my daughter buys me one every Christmas—and I couldn't believe it's already November. The leaves are mostly gone from the oak tree out front. Frank planted that tree when we first bought the house. It was just a little sapling then, barely taller than my knee. He bought it at the nursery down on Main Street, the one that used to be next to the old movie theater. They tore the theater down years ago to build a pharmacy. It's a shame, really. We used to go see double features there for fifty cents. They had real butter on the popcorn, not that artificial yellow stuff they pump out of a machine now. It ruins your clothes if you drop a kernel, stains right through the fabric... 

But, what was I saying? Oh. No, I haven't had a break from it. It's been a solid year and two months of this heavy feeling.

They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this heavy, low feeling you have is something that you experience all the time, or are there times when you don't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: Well, I know I'm sounding like a broken record here, doctor, but it really is all the time. Like I've been saying, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just doesn't leave. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, no. No two months without it. Like I said, it's just always there, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. A whole year and then some. It feels like a lot longer, though. Time is funny like that, isn't it? When you're waiting for something, it drags, but when you look back, it seems to fly by. Like how fast the seasons change. I was just looking at the calendar in my kitchen—the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, my daughter buys me one every Christmas—and I couldn't believe it's already November. The leaves are mostly gone from the oak tree out front. Frank planted that tree when we first bought the house. It was just a little sapling then, barely taller than my knee. He bought it at the nursery down on Main Street, the one that used to be next to the old movie theater. They tore the theater down years ago to build a pharmacy. It's a shame, really. We used to go see double features there for fifty cents. They had real butter on the popcorn, not that artificial yellow stuff they pump out of a machine now. It ruins your clothes if you drop a kernel, stains right through the fabric... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, I haven't had a break from it. It's been a solid year and two months of this heavy feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels like a lot longer, though. Time is funny like that, isn't it? When you're waiting for something, it drags, but when you look back, it seems to fly by. Like how fast the seasons change. I was just looking at the calendar in my kitchen—the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, my daughter buys me one every Christmas—and I couldn't believe it's already November. We used to go see double features there for fifty cents. They had real butter on the popcorn, not that artificial yellow stuff they pump out of a machine now. It ruins your clothes if you drop a kernel, stains right through the fabric... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, I haven't had a break from it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I think we're going in circles a bit! Like I was just saying a second ago, no. That low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months straight now. So there definitely hasn't been any eight-week period in there where I felt like my normal self. 

Eight weeks... you know, that's exactly how long my daughter's dog had to wear one of those plastic lampshade collars. The "cone of shame," she called it. Poor Buster. He's a Golden Retriever, such a sweet boy, but he kept bumping into the doorframes with that thing on. *Thump, thump* every time he walked down the hallway. He'd gotten a terrible scratch on his ear from a rosebush in their backyard. You really have to watch out for rosebushes. I used to have the most beautiful climbing roses on the trellis out back, big yellow ones, but the thorns would catch right on your sweaters if you weren't careful. I ruined a perfectly good cardigan that way once. It was a lovely pale yellow one that my sister knit for me. She was always so good with knitting needles, making baby booties and blankets for everyone in the parish. I tried to learn once, but my tension was always too tight, the stitches would just pucker up and look like a mess... 

But anyway, no. No eight-week breaks. Just that same year and two months of feeling low.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. It's just strange, you know? Some days I forget lunch completely. I'll just be sitting in my armchair—it's this lovely floral pattern, my daughter bought it for me back in, oh, must have been 2015 when she was still living in Ohio before her husband got transferred. They have such terrible winters there, I don't know how they stood it. But anyway, I'll just forget to eat. And then other days I stand at the kitchen counter just eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing tastes right. The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy baking or has your interest in it changed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this heavy, low feeling you have is something that you experience all the time, or are there times when you don't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: Well, I know I'm sounding like a broken record here, doctor, but it really is all the time. Like I've been saying, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just doesn't leave. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, no. No two months without it. Like I said, it's just always there, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. A whole year and then some. It feels like a lot longer, though. Time is funny like that, isn't it? When you're waiting for something, it drags, but when you look back, it seems to fly by. Like how fast the seasons change. I was just looking at the calendar in my kitchen—the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, my daughter buys me one every Christmas—and I couldn't believe it's already November. The leaves are mostly gone from the oak tree out front. Frank planted that tree when we first bought the house. It was just a little sapling then, barely taller than my knee. He bought it at the nursery down on Main Street, the one that used to be next to the old movie theater. They tore the theater down years ago to build a pharmacy. It's a shame, really. We used to go see double features there for fifty cents. They had real butter on the popcorn, not that artificial yellow stuff they pump out of a machine now. It ruins your clothes if you drop a kernel, stains right through the fabric... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, I haven't had a break from it. It's been a solid year and two months of this heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least eight weeks in the past two months where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I think we're going in circles a bit! Like I was just saying a second ago, no. That low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months straight now. So there definitely hasn't been any eight-week period in there where I felt like my normal self. \n\nEight weeks... you know, that's exactly how long my daughter's dog had to wear one of those plastic lampshade collars. The \"cone of shame,\" she called it. Poor Buster. He's a Golden Retriever, such a sweet boy, but he kept bumping into the doorframes with that thing on. *Thump, thump* every time he walked down the hallway. He'd gotten a terrible scratch on his ear from a rosebush in their backyard. You really have to watch out for rosebushes. I used to have the most beautiful climbing roses on the trellis out back, big yellow ones, but the thorns would catch right on your sweaters if you weren't careful. I ruined a perfectly good cardigan that way once. It was a lovely pale yellow one that my sister knit for me. She was always so good with knitting needles, making baby booties and blankets for everyone in the parish. I tried to learn once, but my tension was always too tight, the stitches would just pucker up and look like a mess... \n\nBut anyway, no. No eight-week breaks. Just that same year and two months of feeling low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... I suppose there was a stretch of several weeks in there where my mood had eased up enough that I was eating a bit better. I was actually getting dressed more normally, putting on a proper blouse and slacks instead of just sitting around in my housecoat. But even during those weeks when the heavy feeling lifted a little, I still felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something. 

Records are just so important to keep an eye on, you know. I used to keep all our household records in this heavy metal filing cabinet in the spare room. Frank bought it at a surplus auction back in nineteen eighty-something. It was painted this awful olive green color, like an old army jeep. I tried to cover it with a nice floral contact paper, the kind with the little pink peonies on it, but it kept peeling at the corners. Contact paper never really sticks the way they promise it will on the commercials. I remember watching those commercials during my afternoon stories. *As the World Turns*, that was my favorite. I never missed an episode for thirty years. The drama those people got into, marriages and evil twins and people coming back from the dead... they just don't make television like that anymore. Now it's all "reality" shows, but there's nothing real about them, if you ask me. Just people yelling at each other in fancy houses... 

But yes, to answer you, there were those few weeks where my mood was a bit better, even though the records were still being altered.

The crackers are always those little square ones, but they just taste like cardboard lately. I used to love baking, you know, making cookies and things for the church bake sale, but now... I just stand there eating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy baking or has your interest in it changed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just... I don't care about it anymore. It's like that with everything lately. I used to look forward to so many things, you know? My watercolor class on Tuesdays, the quilting circle, my Thursday book club... I used to have my little canvas tote bag all packed the night before. It's a nice sturdy bag, canvas, with a picture of a bluebird on it that my granddaughter painted for me. She's so talented, she's taking AP Art now at her high school. They have such wonderful programs for the kids these days, not like when I was in school. We just had home economics and typing. I was a very fast typist, actually. But anyway... now I just pack the bag, and I leave it sitting right by the front door. I look at it, and I just can't make myself care enough to actually go out and do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my eating has just gotten all completely turned around. I couldn't tell you an exact number on the weight because I really don't step on the scale much anymore. The scale I have in my bathroom is one of those old dial ones, you know? It's a lovely mint green color. I bought it at Sears back when they still had that big catalog. Oh, I used to love looking through the Sears Wish Book right before Christmas when I was little. My brother and I would circle the things we wanted with a red crayon. He always wanted those heavy metal Tonka trucks, the bright yellow ones. They were practically indestructible, not like the cheap plastic toys they make for kids today... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Right, my weight. I just don't know for sure. Like I said, since I completely forget lunch some days, and then other days I'm just standing at the counter eating sweets and those crackers even though they don't taste right... I might have lost a few pounds, or maybe the sweets balanced it out. I just know my eating is all mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Well, my clothes... I suppose maybe some of my slacks are a bit looser around the waist. They're these elastic-waist ones I got from Blair, you know, they send those catalogs in the mail? They always have such nice pastel colors for spring. I used to order matching twinsets, a cardigan and a little shell underneath. My sister Margaret always said I looked so put together in them. Margaret passed away three years ago, bless her heart. She had the most beautiful rose garden, just bushes and bushes of these deep red tea roses... \n\nBut anyway, like I was telling you earlier, it's just that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I forget lunch completely, and other days I'm just standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets even though nothing tastes right. So I guess maybe that's why my clothes fit a little differently, with my eating being so mixed up like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this... this heavy, low feeling. It's just sitting right there on my chest. It's like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I used to love picking out my clothes in the morning. I had this beautiful floral blouse, a sort of periwinkle blue with little yellow daisies on it. I bought it for my granddaughter's graduation. She wore a yellow dress under her gown, she looked just like a little ray of sunshine. We had the party at that Italian place on Main Street, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. They make the most wonderful garlic knots, though they always give you too much butter. I had to ask for extra napkins. I always keep napkins in my purse, you just never know when you'll need one. My mother taught me that. She always carried a clean handkerchief, ironed perfectly square... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just feeling heavy and empty. It's just... it's there every morning now, before I even get out of bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I just... I don't seem to have the energy for them anymore. I used to look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle, and my book club. We were reading that lovely mystery series set in the English countryside, you know the one? The detective is a vicar's wife, and she always rides this charming old bicycle with a wicker basket on the front. I had a bicycle like that when I was a teenager. It was a Schwinn, a bright cherry red. My father bought it for me for my sixteenth birthday. He tied a big white ribbon on the handlebars. He worked so hard, my father. He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. I can't imagine living in an old factory, with all that exposed brick and those drafty windows. My friend Helen's grandson lives in one, and she says it costs a fortune just to heat the place in the winter...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my classes and things. Yes, I just... I'll get my bag all packed for watercolor or quilting, and I'll leave it right by the front door, but then I just look at it. I just can't make myself care enough to actually go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Well, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. Yes, just over a year. I remember because it started right around the time I bought a new winter coat. I'd been wanting a good, warm wool coat, the kind with the nice satin lining inside. I went down to Macy's, they were having a sale. I always check the Sunday paper for the Macy's coupons. My neighbor, Betty, she gets the paper but she only reads the crossword puzzle, so she gives me the coupon inserts. Betty has a little Yorkie, a sweet little thing named Buster, but he barks at everything. Every time the mail carrier comes, Buster just goes on and on. The mail carrier is a nice young man, always wears those blue shorts even when it's freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't turn blue. My knees always ache when the weather turns cold, especially the left one where I fell on the ice back in '98... \n\nBut, oh, right. The feeling in the mornings. Yes, about a year and two months of waking up with that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is this heavy, low feeling you wake up with every morning always there, or are there some mornings when it's not as bad?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly always there, yes. But... well, I suppose there was a stretch, maybe a few weeks or so, where it wasn't quite as heavy. I felt like the mood had eased up just a little bit, enough that I was actually getting dressed in the mornings again, putting on real clothes instead of just my robe. And I was eating a bit more normally, too. I even made myself a nice bowl of oatmeal with brown sugar and raisins. I always buy the Sun-Maid raisins, the ones in the little red cardboard boxes. My mother used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a little girl. She'd wrap my sandwich in wax paper—people don't use wax paper anymore, do they? Everything is plastic wrap now. It's terrible for the environment, all that plastic just sitting in landfills. I try to recycle, I really do. I rinse out all my tin cans and put them in the blue bin. The recycling truck comes on Thursdays, right after the garbage truck. They make such a racket, those big trucks, all that clanking and grinding...\n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the mornings. Even during those few weeks when I felt a bit lighter, getting dressed and eating... I still couldn't shake this feeling I had. I was just so sure that the dates and the names in the records were being changed. Like someone was going in and altering them to signal something to me. It just felt so clear, even when the sadness wasn't as bad.\n\nDoctor: Are there days when this heavy, low feeling is worse than others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, some days are certainly heavier than others. The worst days... those are the days when I'm really stuck in that low, colorless state. And the funny thing is, when the sadness is at its absolute worst, that's also when I get most caught up in the idea about the papers. I just feel so certain that the family records, the old birth certificates, and even the notes from the hospital... I feel like they're carrying hidden messages meant just for me. Like someone is trying to tell me something through the dates. I keep all those family papers in a cedar chest at the foot of my bed. It's a beautiful chest, solid cedar. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. Tommy had this little Snoopy fishing pole...\n\nBut anyway, yes. The days when the heavy feeling is the worst, that's when the messages in the papers seem the most real to me.\n\nDoctor: Are these heavy, low feelings you wake up with usually still there for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, they do tend to stick around. Most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. I sit on the edge of the bed and look at my slippers... they're those fuzzy pink ones, you know the kind? I got them for Christmas from my niece, Sarah. She lives out in Oregon now, works for a company that makes those reusable water bottles. Everyone carries those water bottles around these days, taking little sips constantly. When I was a girl, we just drank from the water fountain in the hallway and that was enough to last us until dinner. The fountain at my elementary school had this funny metal handle you had to press down really hard. Sometimes the water would shoot up too high and get right up your nose if you weren't careful. My friend Margaret always laughed when that happened. Margaret had the most beautiful red hair, like a shiny copper penny... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about the day? Right, the heavy feeling. Yes, it starts before I even put my slippers on, and it just sort of sits there with me for most of the day, making everything feel so gray and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods during the day when this heavy, low feeling lifts or becomes less intense?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I said before, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just stays like that. It doesn't really lift. It's like a thick fog that just settles in. Fog can be so dangerous, especially when you're driving. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it doesn't really get better as the day goes on. It's just... heavy. Speaking of heavy, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, bought this massive cast-iron skillet at a yard sale last weekend. She brought it over to show me, and I could barely lift the thing! I don't know how she expects to cook with it. Cast iron is so much work, anyway. You have to season it, you can't use soap on it... my mother had a cast-iron Dutch oven she used for everything. Pot roasts, stews, even baked bread in it. She made the best sourdough bread. The starter she had was passed down from her mother, I think. People get so protective of their sourdough starters, giving them names and feeding them like pets. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about a woman who took her sourdough starter on vacation with her so it wouldn't die. Can you imagine? Taking a jar of dough on an airplane? The security people must have thought she was crazy. I haven't been on an airplane since Frank and I went to Florida in '98. The seats were so cramped, and the peanuts were terribly salty... \n\nBut, oh, right. No, the feeling doesn't get better. It just stays heavy all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this heavy, low feeling you have is something that you experience all the time, or are there times when you don't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: Well, I know I'm sounding like a broken record here, doctor, but it really is all the time. Like I've been saying, most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has gone out of the day before I even get dressed. And it just doesn't leave. There aren't any times when it's just gone. It's always there, right from the start of the day. Speaking of the start of the day, the paperboy has been throwing the newspaper right into the hydrangeas lately. I've asked him nicely to leave it on the porch, but I think he just rides his bicycle by so fast he doesn't even look. Kids and their bicycles these days, they go so fast. My brother Tommy had a Schwinn when we were kids, bright blue with those silver tassels on the handlebars. He thought he was the king of the neighborhood on that thing. He used to ride it down Miller's Hill with no hands, which gave my mother absolute fits. She was always so worried about us getting hurt. She used to keep this little brown bottle of iodine in the medicine cabinet that stung like the dickens whenever she put it on a scraped knee. I can still smell it if I think about it hard enough... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, there aren't any times when I don't feel it. It's just that same heavy feeling, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, no. No two months without it. Like I said, it's just always there, that low, colorless stretch... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. A whole year and then some. It feels like a lot longer, though. Time is funny like that, isn't it? When you're waiting for something, it drags, but when you look back, it seems to fly by. Like how fast the seasons change. I was just looking at the calendar in my kitchen—the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, my daughter buys me one every Christmas—and I couldn't believe it's already November. The leaves are mostly gone from the oak tree out front. Frank planted that tree when we first bought the house. It was just a little sapling then, barely taller than my knee. He bought it at the nursery down on Main Street, the one that used to be next to the old movie theater. They tore the theater down years ago to build a pharmacy. It's a shame, really. We used to go see double features there for fifty cents. They had real butter on the popcorn, not that artificial yellow stuff they pump out of a machine now. It ruins your clothes if you drop a kernel, stains right through the fabric... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, I haven't had a break from it. It's been a solid year and two months of this heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least eight weeks in the past two months where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I think we're going in circles a bit! Like I was just saying a second ago, no. That low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months straight now. So there definitely hasn't been any eight-week period in there where I felt like my normal self. \n\nEight weeks... you know, that's exactly how long my daughter's dog had to wear one of those plastic lampshade collars. The \"cone of shame,\" she called it. Poor Buster. He's a Golden Retriever, such a sweet boy, but he kept bumping into the doorframes with that thing on. *Thump, thump* every time he walked down the hallway. He'd gotten a terrible scratch on his ear from a rosebush in their backyard. You really have to watch out for rosebushes. I used to have the most beautiful climbing roses on the trellis out back, big yellow ones, but the thorns would catch right on your sweaters if you weren't careful. I ruined a perfectly good cardigan that way once. It was a lovely pale yellow one that my sister knit for me. She was always so good with knitting needles, making baby booties and blankets for everyone in the parish. I tried to learn once, but my tension was always too tight, the stitches would just pucker up and look like a mess... \n\nBut anyway, no. No eight-week breaks. Just that same year and two months of feeling low.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any specific times or events in the past year and two months when your low feelings significantly improved for more than a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose there was a stretch of several weeks in there where my mood had eased up enough that I was eating a bit better. I was actually getting dressed more normally, putting on a proper blouse and slacks instead of just sitting around in my housecoat. But even during those weeks when the heavy feeling lifted a little, I still felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something. \n\nRecords are just so important to keep an eye on, you know. I used to keep all our household records in this heavy metal filing cabinet in the spare room. Frank bought it at a surplus auction back in nineteen eighty-something. It was painted this awful olive green color, like an old army jeep. I tried to cover it with a nice floral contact paper, the kind with the little pink peonies on it, but it kept peeling at the corners. Contact paper never really sticks the way they promise it will on the commercials. I remember watching those commercials during my afternoon stories. *As the World Turns*, that was my favorite. I never missed an episode for thirty years. The drama those people got into, marriages and evil twins and people coming back from the dead... they just don't make television like that anymore. Now it's all \"reality\" shows, but there's nothing real about them, if you ask me. Just people yelling at each other in fancy houses... \n\nBut yes, to answer you, there were those few weeks where my mood was a bit better, even though the records were still being altered.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, completely free? I don't think so. Looking back on it all, the sadness and that awful loss of interest... they've really taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill. Even during those times when the worries about the records changing were a little quieter, the heavy feeling was still there. 

My sister, you know, she was the one who knit that pale yellow cardigan I told you about. She used to be so full of energy before she got sick. We'd go down to the farmer's market every Saturday morning, rain or shine. She always had to buy her tomatoes from this one specific stall—the man who ran it wore a straw hat with a little red ribbon around the brim. He told us once he got that hat on a trip to Mexico. I've never been to Mexico myself. Frank and I went to Florida once, down to Orlando, right after they opened that big theme park. The humidity there was just terrible, my hair frizzed up like a poodle the second we stepped off the airplane. I had to buy a silk scarf just to keep it somewhat presentable. It was a lovely scarf, though, with little blue sailboats printed all over it. I wonder whatever happened to that scarf... I think it might be in the cedar chest at the foot of my bed. Cedar is so good for keeping the moths away. My mother always swore by cedar blocks in the closets...

But what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the sadness has been there most of the time since she got sick, even when my other worries weren't so loud.

He was a machinist at the factory downtown, the one that made those metal parts for airplanes. The factory closed down years ago, of course. They turned the building into those fancy loft apartments. My late husband, Frank, bought it for our tenth anniversary. He got it at an estate sale. Frank loved estate sales, he'd get up at six in the morning on Saturdays just to be the first one in line. He always said the early bird gets the worm, but I never understood why anyone would want a worm. Unless you're going fishing, I suppose. Frank used to take our nephew Tommy fishing out at the lake. I remember driving up to the mountains with Frank one autumn, and the fog was so thick you couldn't see the hood of the car. We had to pull over at this little diner on the side of the highway. They had the best cherry pie there. The crust was so flaky, not like the frozen ones you buy at the supermarket nowadays. Those always taste like cardboard, don't you think? I tried to make a pie crust from scratch once, using my grandmother's recipe. It called for lard, which I know isn't good for your cholesterol, but it really does make a difference in the texture. My doctor is always telling me to watch my cholesterol, telling me to eat more oatmeal. Which I do, with the raisins, like I mentioned... \n\nBut no, the heavy feeling doesn't really go away. It just stays with me, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Are there ever any moments during the day when you feel slightly better than when you first wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I mean, I know I've said this a few times now, but most mornings I wake up with that heavy, low feeling, like the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. He bought it at the nursery down on Main Street, the one that used to be next to the old movie theater. They tore the theater down years ago to build a pharmacy. It's a shame, really. I was actually getting dressed more normally, putting on a proper blouse and slacks instead of just sitting around in my housecoat. I used to keep all our household records in this heavy metal filing cabinet in the spare room. Frank bought it at a surplus auction back in nineteen eighty-something. It was painted this awful olive green color, like an old army jeep. I tried to cover it with a nice floral contact paper, the kind with the little pink peonies on it, but it kept peeling at the corners. Contact paper never really sticks the way they promise it will on the commercials. I remember watching those commercials during my afternoon stories. *As the World Turns*, that was my favorite. I never missed an episode for thirty years. The drama those people got into, marriages and evil twins and people coming back from the dead... they just don't make television like that anymore. Now it's all \"reality\" shows, but there's nothing real about them, if you ask me. Just people yelling at each other in fancy houses... \n\nBut yes, to answer you, there were those few weeks where my mood was a bit better, even though the records were still being altered.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, no matter how small, where you felt completely free of this low, heavy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, completely free? I don't think so. Looking back on it all, the sadness and that awful loss of interest... they've really taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill. Even during those times when the worries about the records changing were a little quieter, the heavy feeling was still there. \n\nMy sister, you know, she was the one who knit that pale yellow cardigan I told you about. She used to be so full of energy before she got sick. We'd go down to the farmer's market every Saturday morning, rain or shine. She always had to buy her tomatoes from this one specific stall—the man who ran it wore a straw hat with a little red ribbon around the brim. He told us once he got that hat on a trip to Mexico. I've never been to Mexico myself. Frank and I went to Florida once, down to Orlando, right after they opened that big theme park. The humidity there was just terrible, my hair frizzed up like a poodle the second we stepped off the airplane. I had to buy a silk scarf just to keep it somewhat presentable. It was a lovely scarf, though, with little blue sailboats printed all over it. I wonder whatever happened to that scarf... I think it might be in the cedar chest at the foot of my bed. But even during those weeks when the heavy feeling lifted a little, I still felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something. \n\nRecords are just so important to keep an eye on, you know. I used to keep all our household records in this heavy metal filing cabinet in the spare room. Frank bought it at a surplus auction back in nineteen eighty-something. It was painted this awful olive green color, like an old army jeep. I tried to cover it with a nice floral contact paper, the kind with the little pink peonies on it, but it kept peeling at the corners. Contact paper never really sticks the way they promise it will on the commercials. I remember watching those commercials during my afternoon stories. *As the World Turns*, that was my favorite. I never missed an episode for thirty years. The drama those people got into, marriages and evil twins and people coming back from the dead... they just don't make television like that anymore. Now it's all \"reality\" shows, but there's nothing real about them, if you ask me. Just people yelling at each other in fancy houses... \n\nBut yes, to answer you, there were those few weeks where my mood was a bit better, even though the records were still being altered.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, no matter how small, where you felt completely free of this low, heavy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, completely free? I don't think so. Looking back on it all, the sadness and that awful loss of interest... they've really taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill. Even during those times when the worries about the records changing were a little quieter, the heavy feeling was still there. \n\nMy sister, you know, she was the one who knit that pale yellow cardigan I told you about. She used to be so full of energy before she got sick. We'd go down to the farmer's market every Saturday morning, rain or shine. She always had to buy her tomatoes from this one specific stall—the man who ran it wore a straw hat with a little red ribbon around the brim. He told us once he got that hat on a trip to Mexico. I've never been to Mexico myself. Frank and I went to Florida once, down to Orlando, right after they opened that big theme park. The humidity there was just terrible, my hair frizzed up like a poodle the second we stepped off the airplane. I had to buy a silk scarf just to keep it somewhat presentable. It was a lovely scarf, though, with little blue sailboats printed all over it. I wonder whatever happened to that scarf... I think it might be in the cedar chest at the foot of my bed. Cedar is so good for keeping the moths away. My mother always swore by cedar blocks in the closets...\n\nBut what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the sadness has been there most of the time since she got sick, even when my other worries weren't so loud.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had two or more years where you felt low or depressed most days, even if it wasn't as severe as what you're experiencing now?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.
